**Sir Keir Rodney Starmer**[^cite_note-1] (born 2 September 1962) is a British politician and former lawyer who served as [Prime Minister of the United Kingdom](24150.md) from 2024 to 2026. He was [Leader of the Labour Party](19043368.md) from 2020 to 2026 and [Leader of the Opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_\(United_Kingdom\)) from 2020 to 2024. He was the [Member of Parliament](3776360.md) (MP) for [Holborn and St Pancras](1344871.md) from 2015 to 2026. Before entering politics, he was a barrister and served as [Director of Public Prosecutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Director_of_Public_Prosecutions_\(England_and_Wales\)) from 2008 to 2013.

Born in [Southwark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwark), London, and raised in [Surrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrey), Starmer was politically active for the [Labour Party Young Socialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_Party_Young_Socialists) as a teenager. He graduated with a [Bachelor of Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor_of_Laws) from the [University of Leeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Leeds) in 1985 and received a [Bachelor of Civil Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor_of_Civil_Law) degree from [St Edmund Hall, Oxford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Edmund_Hall,_Oxford) in 1986. After being [called to the bar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Called_to_the_bar), Starmer worked predominantly in criminal defence, specialising in [human rights](13831.md). He was a human rights adviser to the [Northern Ireland Policing Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Policing_Board), becoming a [Queen's Counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen's_Counsel) in 2002. During his tenure as [Director of Public Prosecutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Director_of_Public_Prosecutions_\(England_and_Wales\)) he handled cases including the [Stephen Lawrence murder case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_Stephen_Lawrence). In the [2014 New Year Honours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_New_Year_Honours), he was appointed a [Knight Commander of the Order of the Bath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knight_Commander_of_the_Order_of_the_Bath) (KCB) for services to law and criminal justice.

Starmer was elected to the [House of Commons](13828.md) at the [2015 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_United_Kingdom_general_election). He supported the [Remain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remain_\(Brexit\)) campaign in the [2016 European Union membership referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_European_Union_membership_referendum) and advocated for [a second referendum on Brexit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposed_referendum_on_the_Brexit_withdrawal_agreement). He served under [Jeremy Corbyn](415036.md) as [Shadow Brexit Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Brexit_Secretary) and, following Corbyn's resignation after Labour's defeat at the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election), Starmer won the [2020 party leadership election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Labour_Party_leadership_election_\(UK\)). As Leader of the Opposition, he moved the [Labour Party](19279158.md) towards the [political centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_centre) and claimed to have eliminated [antisemitism within the party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_the_British_Labour_Party), but was criticised for breaking his leadership campaign pledges. His party made significant gains at the [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_United_Kingdom_local_elections) and [2024 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_local_elections) despite a drop in membership.

Starmer led Labour to a victory at the [2024 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election), ending 14 years of [Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) government with the smallest national vote share of any [majority government](465640.md) since record-keeping began [in 1830](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1830_United_Kingdom_general_election). Under [Starmer's premiership](77280609.md), [his government](77280613.md) ended [Winter Fuel Payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_Fuel_Payment) for some pensioners, implemented an early-release scheme for prisoners to decrease [prison overcrowding](76650342.md), and launched a National Violent Disorder Programme following [nationwide riots in 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_riots). Starmer restricted [visa conditions](28258710.md), closed legal migration routes, and launched the [Border Security Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Security_Command). His government also established [Great British Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_British_Energy), decriminalised [abortion in England and Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_and_Policing_Act_2026), and announced changes to workers' and renters' rights, an increase in the [minimum wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_wage) and investment in [a new nuclear power station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sizewell_C). In foreign policy, Starmer continued [support for Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_and_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) in the [Russo-Ukrainian war](42085878.md) and [qualified support for Israel in Gaza](76740255.md) while [formally recognising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recognition_of_Palestine) the [State of Palestine](241405.md). His government negotiated [free trade](10883.md) agreements [with India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_Comprehensive_Economic_and_Trade_Agreement), the [European Union](9317.md) and the [United States](3434750.md), and sought to improve [relations with China](2612558.md).

Starmer proved unpopular with the British public. [His net approval rating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leadership_approval_opinion_polling_for_the_next_United_Kingdom_general_election#Leadership_approval) began slightly positive but fell over the course of his premiership to an average of −46% by November 2025; an [Ipsos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipsos) poll that month indicates he was the least popular prime minister since its records began in 1977. Starmer also presided over deepening unpopularity towards Labour and a period of left-wing exodus from the party to the [Green Party](79787.md). Labour saw record electoral losses during the [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_United_Kingdom_local_elections) and [2026 local elections](79139854.md), which culminated in [a leadership crisis](83157483.md) and his resignation. Starmer tendered his resignation on 20 July 2026, and was succeeded by [Andy Burnham](414818.md). On 1 September 2026, he resigned as an MP, triggering the [2026 Holborn and St Pancras by-election](84114410.md).

## Early life

Keir Rodney Starmer was born on 2 September 1962 in [Southwark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwark), south-east London,[^cite_note-Anon-2024-2][^cite_note-The_Daily_Telegraph-2024-3][^cite_note-4] and raised in [Oxted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxted), Surrey.[^cite_note-5][^cite_note-6][^cite_note-Maguire-2020-7] He is the second of four children of Josephine ([née](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_name#Maiden_and_married_names) Baker), a nurse, and Rodney Starmer, a [toolmaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toolmaker).[^cite_note-Maguire-2020-7][^cite_note-8] His mother developed [Still's disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adult-onset_Still's_disease).[^cite_note-Stewart-2020-9][^cite_note-The_Daily_Telegraph-2024-3] She attended St John's Anglican Church in nearby Hurst Green, while his father was an atheist.[^cite_note-10] He was nominally "brought up [Church of England](5955.md)".[^cite_note-11] His parents were both Labour Party supporters, and reputedly named him after the party's first parliamentary leader, [Keir Hardie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keir_Hardie),[^cite_note-Bates-2008-12][^cite_note-Moss-2009-13] although Starmer did not confirm this when asked in 2015.[^cite_note-14]

Starmer passed the [11-plus examination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/11-plus) and gained entry to [Reigate Grammar School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reigate_Grammar_School), which at the time was a [voluntary-aided](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voluntary-aided) [selective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selective_school) [grammar school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammar_school).[^cite_note-Anon-2024-2][^cite_note-Moss-2009-13] The school converted into an [independent fee-paying school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_schools_in_the_United_Kingdom) in 1976, while he was a student. The terms of the conversion were such that his parents were not required to pay for his schooling until he turned 16, and when he reached that point, the school, by now a charity, awarded him a [bursary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bursary) that allowed him to complete his education there without any parental contribution.[^cite_note-15][^cite_note-16][^cite_note-17] The subjects he chose to study in the sixth form during his last two years at school were mathematics, music, and physics, in which he achieved [A level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_level) grades of B, B, and C.[^cite_note-18] Among his classmates at Reigate were the musician Norman Cook ([Fatboy Slim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatboy_Slim)), with whom Starmer took violin lessons; [Andrew Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Cooper,_Baron_Cooper_of_Windrush), who later became a Conservative peer; and the future conservative journalist [Andrew Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Sullivan). According to Starmer, he and Sullivan "fought over everything ... Politics, religion. You name it."[^cite_note-Maguire-2020-7]

## Legal career

After being [called to the bar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Called_to_the_bar), Starmer worked predominantly in criminal defence, specialising in [human rights](13831.md). He served as a human rights adviser to the [Northern Ireland Policing Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Policing_Board), taking silk as a [Queen's Counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen's_Counsel) in 2002. During his tenure as [Director of Public Prosecutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Director_of_Public_Prosecutions_\(England_and_Wales\)) and [head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_prosecutor) of the [Crown Prosecution Service](383064.md), he handled major cases including the [Stephen Lawrence murder case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_Stephen_Lawrence). In the [2014 New Year Honours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_New_Year_Honours), he was appointed a [Knight Commander of the Order of the Bath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knight_Commander_of_the_Order_of_the_Bath) (KCB) for services to law and criminal justice.

Starmer became a [barrister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barrister) in 1987 at the [Middle Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Temple), then a [bencher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bencher) in 2009.[^cite_note-Anon-2024-2][^cite_note-The_Daily_Telegraph-2024-3] He served as a legal officer for the campaign group [Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_\(advocacy_group\)) until 1990.[^cite_note-Bates-2008-12] After joining the [Haldane Society of Socialist Lawyers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haldane_Society_of_Socialist_Lawyers), he was its secretary[^cite_note-BK-19] (1988–92[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21]), treasurer (1992–95[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23]) and executive committee member (1996–99[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-25]). As part of his involvement in the Haldane Society, he joined an academic trip to the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) on the eve of its collapse in 1991, meeting the Russian chief justice [Vyacheslav Lebedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vyacheslav_Lebedev_\(jurist\)), led the society's delegation to [Northern Ireland](21265.md) during [the Troubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Troubles), and organised the UK delegation to the 14th Congress of the [International Association of Democratic Lawyers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Association_of_Democratic_Lawyers) in [Cape Town](6653.md) in 1996, meeting President [Nelson Mandela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Mandela).[^cite_note-BK-19][^cite_note-26] Starmer joined the newly founded [Doughty Street Chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doughty_Street_Chambers) in 1990 and worked primarily on human rights matters.[^cite_note-Stewart-2020-9][^cite_note-Bates-2008-12][^cite_note-BK-19]

Starmer has been [called to the Bar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Called_to_the_Bar) in several [Caribbean countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caribbean_Community),[^cite_note-27] where he defended convicts sentenced to the death penalty.[^cite_note-Maguire-2020-7] In 1999, he was a junior barrister on [Lee Clegg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Clegg)'s [appeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appeal).[^cite_note-28] Starmer assisted [Helen Steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helen_Steel) and David Morris in the [McLibel case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLibel_case), at the trial and appeal in English courts, also representing them before the [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights).[^cite_note-29] Starmer was appointed [Queen's Counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen's_Counsel) on 9 April 2002, aged 39.[^cite_note-30] In the same year, he became joint head of Doughty Street Chambers. In 2005 Starmer called his Queen's Counsel appointment "odd" as he had previously expressed support for the [abolition of the monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism_in_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-31]

Starmer wrote legal opinions and marched in protest against the [Iraq War](5043324.md) following the [2003 invasion of Iraq](201936.md), and said in 2015 that he believed the war was "[not lawful under international law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legality_of_the_Iraq_War) because there was no UN resolution expressly authorising it".[^cite_note-32][^cite_note-Maguire-2020-7] He defended one of the [Fairford Five](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fairford_Five) who broke into the [RAF Fairford](475013.md) military air base in 2003 and disabled equipment in order to disrupt military operations at the start of the Iraq War.[^cite_note-33]

## Human rights defender

Starmer served as a [human rights defender](4358082.md) to the [Northern Ireland Policing Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Policing_Board) and the [Association of Chief Police Officers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Chief_Police_Officers), and was also a member of the [Foreign, Commonwealth and Development Office](59653.md)'s [Death Penalty](5902.md) Advisory Panel from 2002 to 2008.[^cite_note-The_Daily_Telegraph-2024-3][^cite_note-Bates-2008-12] The Northern Ireland Board was an important part of bringing communities together following the [Good Friday Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Friday_Agreement), and Starmer later cited his work on policing in Northern Ireland as being a key influence on his decision to pursue a political career: "Some of the things I thought that needed to change in police services we achieved more quickly than we achieved in strategic litigation ... I came better to understand how you can change by being inside and getting the trust of people".[^cite_note-Maguire-2020-7]

Starmer represented Croatia at the genocide hearings before the [International Court of Justice](14918.md) at [The Hague](30269.md) in 2014, arguing that Serbia wanted to seize a third of Croatian territory during the [Croatian War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croatian_War_of_Independence) and eradicate the Croatian population.[^cite_note-34]

## Director of Public Prosecutions



Official portrait as [Director of Public Prosecutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Director_of_Public_Prosecutions_\(England_and_Wales\)), 2009

In July 2008 [Baroness Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroness_Scotland), the [Attorney General for England and Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_for_England_and_Wales), named Starmer as the new Head of the [Crown Prosecution Service](383064.md) (CPS) and [Director of Public Prosecutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Director_of_Public_Prosecutions_\(England_and_Wales\)) (DPP). He succeeded [Ken Macdonald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Macdonald), who publicly welcomed Starmer's appointment, on 1 November 2008.[^cite_note-Bates-2008-12][^cite_note-Moss-2009-13] Starmer was deemed to be bringing a focus on human rights into the legal system.[^cite_note-Bates-2008-12] In 2011 he introduced changes that included the "first test paperless hearing".[^cite_note-35] During his time as DPP Starmer dealt with a number of major cases including the [Stephen Lawrence murder case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_Stephen_Lawrence), where he brought his murderers to justice.[^cite_note-36]

The United states attorney general [Eric Holder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Holder) reportedly secured Starmer's support for the extradition of the Scottish IT expert [Gary McKinnon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_McKinnon), who in 2001 had hacked into US military databases looking for information on [Unidentified Flying Objects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unidentified_Flying_Objects) (UFOs), on an interdict that could have led to a seventy-year jail sentence. The extradition was eventually blocked by [Theresa May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theresa_May) during [her premiership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premiership_of_Theresa_May).[^cite_note-37]

In February 2010, Starmer announced the CPS's decision to prosecute three Labour MPs and a Conservative peer for offences relating to false accounting in the aftermath of the [parliamentary expenses scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_parliamentary_expenses_scandal); all were found guilty.[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] Starmer prioritised rapid prosecutions of rioters over long sentences during the [2011 England riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_England_riots), which he later concluded helped to bring "the situation back under control".[^cite_note-40][^cite_note-41] In February 2012 Starmer announced that [Chris Huhne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Huhne) would be prosecuted for [perverting the course of justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R_v_Huhne)*,* stating in relation to the case that "\[w]here there is sufficient evidence we do not shy away from prosecuting politicians".[^cite_note-42]

In 2012 the journalist [Nick Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Cohen) published allegations that Starmer was personally responsible for allowing the prosecution of Paul Chambers to proceed, in what became known as the "[Twitter joke trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter_joke_trial)". The CPS denied that Starmer was behind the decision, saying that it was the responsibility of a Crown Court and was out of Starmer's hands.[^cite_note-43] When [Jimmy Savile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Savile)'s sexual abuse crimes were exposed in 2012, Starmer said amid [the subsequent scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Savile_sexual_abuse_scandal) that "It was like a dam had bust and people rightfully wanted to know why he had been allowed to get away with it for so long."[^cite_note-44] In 2013 Starmer announced changes to how sexual abuse investigations were to be handled amid [Operation Yewtree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Yewtree), including a panel to review complaints.[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46]

Starmer stepped down as Director of Public Prosecutions in November 2013, and was succeeded by [Alison Saunders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alison_Saunders).[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48] Starmer was awarded several honorary degrees between 2011 and 2014, and was appointed [Knight Commander of the Order of the Bath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knight_Commander_of_the_Order_of_the_Bath) (KCB) in the [2014 New Year Honours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_New_Year_Honours) for "services to law and criminal justice".[^cite_note-Atwal-2013-49][^cite_note-The_London_Gazette-2013-50]

## Member of Parliament



Official portrait as MP, 2017

> My predecessor, the Right Hon. Frank Dobson, to whom I pay tribute, was a powerful advocate of the rights of everyone in Holborn and St Pancras throughout his highly distinguished parliamentary career. Widely respected and widely regarded, he served the people of Holborn and St Pancras for 36 years. Although I doubt I will clock up 36 years, I intend to follow in Frank Dobson's footsteps—albeit my jokes are likely to seem tame when compared with his, and I might give the beard a miss.

— Keir Starmer in his [maiden speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maiden_speech) to the House of Commons, May 2015

Starmer was selected in December 2014 as the Labour parliamentary candidate for the [constituency](1784582.md) of [Holborn and St Pancras](1344871.md), a Labour [safe seat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safe_seat), following the decision of [Frank Dobson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Dobson), its sitting MP, to retire.[^cite_note-51] Starmer was elected at the [2015 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_United_Kingdom_general_election) with a majority of 17,048 (receiving 52.9 per cent of the vote).[^cite_note-52] He was returned at the [2017 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_United_Kingdom_general_election) with an increased majority of 30,509 (70.1 per cent) at the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election) with a reduced majority of 27,763 (64.9 per cent), and at the [2024 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election) with a further reduced majority of 18,884 (48.9 per cent).

During the [2016 European Union membership referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_European_Union_membership_referendum), Starmer supported the [Britain Stronger in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britain_Stronger_in_Europe) campaign for the UK to remain in the [European Union](9317.md) (EU).[^cite_note-53] A member of the parliamentary groups [Labour Friends of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_Friends_of_Israel) and [Labour Friends of Palestine and the Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_Friends_of_Palestine_and_the_Middle_East),[^cite_note-54] Starmer was urged by a number of activists to stand in the [2015 Labour Party leadership election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Labour_Party_leadership_election_\(UK\)) following the resignation of [Ed Miliband](1290018.md) as [Leader of the Labour Party](19043368.md) after Labour's defeat at the [2015 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_United_Kingdom_general_election); he ruled this out, citing his relative lack of political experience at the time.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56] During the leadership election Starmer supported [Andy Burnham](414818.md), who finished second to [Jeremy Corbyn](415036.md).[^cite_note-57]

## Shadow portfolios



Starmer and Jeremy Corbyn, 6 December 2019

Starmer was appointed to [Corbyn's Shadow Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corbyn's_shadow_cabinet) as Shadow Home Office Minister in September 2015. He resigned from this role in June 2016 as part of the mass [Shadow Cabinet resignations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_British_shadow_cabinet_resignations) in protest at [Corbyn's leadership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corbyn's_leadership) following the 2016 EU Referendum result.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] Following [Corbyn's re-election as leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Labour_Party_leadership_election_\(UK\)) in September 2016, Starmer accepted a new post from Corbyn as [Shadow Secretary of State for Exiting the European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Secretary_of_State_for_Exiting_the_European_Union).[^cite_note-60] In this role, Starmer questioned May and [her first ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_May_ministry)'s destination for the UK outside of the EU, as well as calling for [Brexit](41688778.md) plans to be made public and supporting a [proposed second referendum on Brexit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposed_referendum_on_the_Brexit_withdrawal_agreement).[^cite_note-61] Following Labour's defeat at the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election), Corbyn announced that he would not lead Labour at the [next general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election) after "a process of reflection".[^cite_note-62] Starmer began to distance himself from Corbyn's leadership and many of the policies put forward at the general election, later saying in 2024 that he was "certain that we would lose the 2019 election".[^cite_note-63]

## Labour leadership campaign



Starmer speaking at a leadership election hustings

Main article: [2020 Labour Party leadership election (UK)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Labour_Party_leadership_election_\(UK\))



Starmer's logo for the Labour leadership

On 4 January 2020 Starmer announced his candidacy for the resultant [leadership election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Labour_Party_leadership_election_\(UK\)).[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65] He gained support from the former Labour prime minister [Gordon Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_Brown) and [London Mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Mayor) [Sadiq Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadiq_Khan).[^cite_note-winner-66] During the Labour leadership campaign, Starmer ran a [left-wing](18499.md) platform and positioned himself in opposition to [austerity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_government_austerity_programme), stating that Corbyn was right to position Labour as "the party of [anti-austerity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-austerity)".[^cite_note-New_European2-67][^cite_note-68] He indicated he would continue with the Labour policy of scrapping [tuition fees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuition_fees_in_the_United_Kingdom) as well as pledging "common ownership" of rail, mail, energy and water companies, and called for ending outsourcing in the NHS, local government and the justice system.[^cite_note-69] Starmer was declared the winner of Labour's leadership contest on 4 April 2020, defeating his rivals, [Rebecca Long-Bailey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebecca_Long-Bailey) and [Lisa Nandy](27266258.md), with 56.2 per cent of the vote in the first round.[^cite_note-70][^cite_note-71][^cite_note-winner-66]

> It is the honour and the privilege of my life to be elected as Leader of the Labour Party. I want to thank Rebecca and Lisa for running such passionate and powerful campaigns and for their friendship and support along the way. I want to thank our Labour Party staff who worked really hard and my own amazing campaign team, full of positivity, with that unifying spirit. I want to pay tribute to Jeremy Corbyn, who led our party through some really difficult times, who energised our movement and who's a friend as well as a colleague. And to all of our members, supporters and affiliates I say this: whether you voted for me or not I will represent you, I will listen to you and I will bring our party together.

— Keir Starmer's acceptance speech, April 2020

## Leader of the Opposition (2020–2024)

Main article: [Keir Starmer as Leader of the Opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keir_Starmer_as_Leader_of_the_Opposition)



Starmer speaking in the [Commons](13828.md) during [Prime Minister's Questions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister's_Questions), 22 April 2020

Having become Leader of the Opposition during the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_Kingdom), Starmer said in his acceptance speech that he would refrain from "scoring party political points" and would work with the Government "in the national interest".[^cite_note-72] He later became more critical of [HM Government's response to the pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_government_response_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic) following the [Partygate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partygate) scandal.[^cite_note-73] On 13 December 2021, during the COVID-19 pandemic in the United Kingdom, Starmer recorded a Christmas message at [Waheed Alli, Baron Alli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waheed_Alli,_Baron_Alli)'s house, urging the public to [work from home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Work_from_home), while giving the impression he was in his own home, with a family picture behind him.[^cite_note-74] In May 2022, Starmer said he would resign were he to receive a [fixed penalty notice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fixed_penalty_notice) for breaching COVID-19 regulations while campaigning during the run-up to the [Hartlepool by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Hartlepool_by-election) and [local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Kingdom_local_elections) the previous year.[^cite_note-75] The controversy surrounding the event was dubbed "[Beergate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beergate)".[^cite_note-76] In July 2022, Durham Police cleared Starmer and said that he had "no case to answer".[^cite_note-77] In the following month, the [Parliamentary Commissioner for Standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_Commissioner_for_Standards), [Kathryn Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathryn_Stone), found that Starmer had breached the MPs' code of conduct on eight occasions by failing to register interests.[^cite_note-\:0-78][^cite_note-79]

Amidst the [historic number of ministers resigning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_2022_United_Kingdom_government_crisis) from [Boris Johnson](19065069.md)'s government in July 2022, Starmer proposed a [vote of no confidence in the Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_vote_of_confidence_in_the_Johnson_ministry), stating that Johnson should not be allowed to remain in office.[^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81] Starmer also criticised Johnson, as well as his successors [Liz Truss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liz_Truss) and [Rishi Sunak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rishi_Sunak), for issues such as the [Chris Pincher scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Pincher_scandal) and the subsequent government crisis,[^cite_note-82] the economic crisis resulting from the [2022 mini-budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_2022_United_Kingdom_mini-budget) and [subsequent government crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2022_United_Kingdom_government_crisis),[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85] the [cost of living crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021%E2%80%93present_United_Kingdom_cost-of-living_crisis),[^cite_note-86] and the [industrial disputes and strikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_industrial_disputes_and_strikes_\(2022%E2%80%93present\)) including [National Health Service strikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%93present_National_Health_Service_strikes).[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89]

As Labour leader Starmer focused on repositioning the Party away from [the Left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_left) and the controversies that affected Corbyn's leadership, with promises of economic stability, tackling [small-boat crossings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Channel_migrant_crossings_\(2018%E2%80%93present\)), cutting NHS waiting times and "rebuilding the NHS", worker rights enrichment, [energy independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_British_Energy) and infrastructure development, tackling crime, improving education and training, reforming public services, renationalising the railway network, and recruiting 6,500 teachers.[^cite_note-90] Starmer also pledged to end [antisemitism within the Labour Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_the_British_Labour_Party).[^cite_note-winner-66][^cite_note-91] In October 2020, following the release of the [Equality and Human Rights Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_and_Human_Rights_Commission) (EHRC)'s [report into antisemitism in the Labour Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_work_of_the_Labour_Party's_Governance_and_Legal_Unit_in_relation_to_antisemitism,_2014%E2%80%932019), Starmer accepted its findings in full and apologised to Jews on the Party's behalf.[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93] In February 2023 the EHRC said that changes the party had made to its complaints and training procedures meant that the Labour Party no longer needed to be monitored by them.[^cite_note-94]

In September 2023 he reshuffled his shadow cabinet.[^cite_note-95] Starmer was ranked number two in the *[New Statesman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Statesman)'*&#x73; Left Power List 2023, below his [Shadow Chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Chancellor), [Rachel Reeves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Reeves), but still described as "the overwhelming favourite to be the next Prime Minister".[^cite_note-96] The reshuffle was seen as a promotion of [Blairites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blairites) and demotion of those on the [soft left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_left).[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-cunliffe-2023-98][^cite_note-chaplain-2023-99]

During the 2023 [Gaza War](74998519.md), Starmer emphasised his support for Israel, stated he would favour military aid to the country, and called the actions of [Hamas](13913.md) and other militants terrorism.[^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101] In an interview with [LBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LBC) on 11 October 2023, Starmer was asked whether it would be appropriate for Israel to [totally cut off power and water supplies to Gaza](75013719.md), with Starmer replying that "I think that Israel does have that right" and that "obviously everything should be done within international law".[^cite_note-lbc-2025-102][^cite_note-103] On 20 October, Starmer clarified that he only meant that Israel had the right to defend itself.[^cite_note-sky-2023-104] Starmer had said that a ceasefire would better allow Hamas to make future attacks; instead, he called for a humanitarian pause to allow aid to reach Gaza.[^cite_note-105]

On 15 November 2023, Starmer suffered his largest defeat as leader when 56 of his MPs (including ten frontbenchers) defied a [three-line whip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three-line_whip) in voting for an SNP motion to support an immediate ceasefire in Gaza.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107] In December 2023, Keir Starmer followed Rishi Sunak in changing his stance by calling for a "sustainable ceasefire" in relation to the conflict in Gaza. This also came after the [Foreign Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Secretary_\(United_Kingdom\)) [David Cameron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Cameron)'s same change in position. Starmer stated his support for a "two-stage" "two-state solution".[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110] During Starmer's tenure as leader, Labour saw a drop in party membership from a peak of 532,000 after the 2019 election to 370,450 in the runup to the 2024 election.[^cite_note-111] More than 20,000 members left the party within two months in 2024, with blame placed on the party's stance on the [Gazan genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gazan_genocide) and [green investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_investment).[^cite_note-112]

### Shadow cabinet

Main article: [Shadow Cabinet of Keir Starmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Cabinet_of_Keir_Starmer)

Starmer's [shadow cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_cabinet) initially comprised both the [right](56522.md) and [left](18499.md) of the Labour Party. Starmer [reshuffled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reshuffled) his shadow cabinet three times – firstly in [May 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2021_British_shadow_cabinet_reshuffle), secondly in [November 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2021_British_shadow_cabinet_reshuffle), and finally in [September 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_British_shadow_cabinet_reshuffle).[^cite_note-cunliffe-2023-98][^cite_note-chaplain-2023-99] Starmer's reshuffles reduced the representation of the left and [soft left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_left) on the opposition [frontbench](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frontbench), while increasing the representation of the party's right.[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116] Notable changes included [Rachel Reeves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Reeves) replacing [Anneliese Dodds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anneliese_Dodds) as [Shadow Chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Chancellor), the demotion of [Lisa Nandy](27266258.md) from [Shadow Levelling-Up Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Secretary_of_State_for_Housing,_Communities_and_Local_Government) to [Shadow Minister for International Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Minister_for_International_Development), and the replacement of [Chief Whip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Whip_of_the_Labour_Party) [Nick Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Brown) with [Alan Campbell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Campbell_\(politician\)). Resignations from Starmer's shadow cabinet included [Andy McDonald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_McDonald_\(politician\)) and [Rosena Allin-Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosena_Allin-Khan).

### Local election results

See also: [2021 United Kingdom local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Kingdom_local_elections), [2022 United Kingdom local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_Kingdom_local_elections), [2023 United Kingdom local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_United_Kingdom_local_elections), and [2024 United Kingdom local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_local_elections)

Starmer considered resigning after Labour's mixed results in the 2021 local elections, the first local elections of his leadership, but later felt "vindicated" by his decision to stay on, saying, "I did \[consider quitting] because I didn't feel that I should be bigger than the party and that if I couldn't bring about the change, perhaps there should be a change. But actually, in the end, I reflected on it, talked to very many people and doubled down and determined, no, it is the change in the Labour Party we need".[^cite_note-117]

During Starmer's tenure as Opposition Leader, his party suffered the loss of a previously safe Labour seat at the [2021 Hartlepool by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Hartlepool_by-election), followed by holds at the [2021 Batley and Spen by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Batley_and_Spen_by-election), [2022 Birmingham Erdington by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Birmingham_Erdington_by-election) and [2022 City of Chester by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_City_of_Chester_by-election), as well as a gain from the Conservatives at the [2022 Wakefield by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Wakefield_by-election). During the [2023 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_United_Kingdom_local_elections), Labour gained more than 500 councillors and 22 councils, becoming the largest party in local government for the first time since [2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_United_Kingdom_local_elections).[^cite_note-118] Labour made further gains at the [2024 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_local_elections), gaining from the Conservatives at the [Blackpool South by-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackpool_South_by-election) and narrowly winning the [West Midlands mayoral election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_West_Midlands_mayoral_election).[^cite_note-119]

### 2024 general election

Main article: [2024 United Kingdom general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election)

On 22 May 2024, Prime Minister [Rishi Sunak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rishi_Sunak) announced that a general election would be held on 4 July 2024. Labour entered the general election with a large lead over the Conservatives in [opinion polls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling_for_the_2024_United_Kingdom_general_election) (which had been the case since 2022), and the potential scale of the party's victory remained a topic of discussion throughout the campaign.[^cite_note-120][^cite_note-121]

In June 2024 Starmer released the Labour Party's 2024 manifesto, *[Change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Change_\(manifesto\))*, which focused on economic growth, planning system changes, infrastructure, what Starmer describes as "clean energy", healthcare, education, childcare, and strengthening workers' rights.[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123] It pledged a new publicly owned energy company ([Great British Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_British_Energy)), a "Green Prosperity Plan", reducing patient waiting times in the NHS, and [renationalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renationalisation) of the [railway network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railways_in_the_United_Kingdom) ([Great British Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_British_Railways)).[^cite_note-124] Promising wealth creation together with "pro-business and pro-worker" policies,[^cite_note-125] the manifesto also pledged giving 16-year-olds the vote in all elections, reforming the [House of Lords](13658.md), and to tax [private schools](612857.md), with money generated going into improving state education.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127] On taxes, the day after the manifesto was released, Starmer pledged that not only would income tax, [National Insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Insurance), and VAT not be increased, but that, per their manifesto, their plans were fully costed and funded and would not require tax increases.[^cite_note-128]

Starmer led Labour to a landslide victory at the general election, characterised by commentators as a "loveless landslide", ending fourteen years of Conservative government with Labour becoming the largest party in the House of Commons.[^cite_note-129][^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132] Labour achieved a 174-seat simple majority and a total of 411 seats, the party's third-best ever result in terms of seat-share (following the [1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_United_Kingdom_general_election) and [2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_United_Kingdom_general_election) general elections). The party became the largest in England for the first time since [2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_United_Kingdom_general_election), [in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election_in_Scotland) for the first time [since 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_United_Kingdom_general_election_in_Scotland) and retained its status as the largest party [in Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election_in_Wales).[^cite_note-133] Despite this, Labour won 34 per cent of the vote – the lowest of any party forming a majority government in the post-war era,[^cite_note-134] leading to concerns about the [proportionality of the election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_general_election#Proportionality_concerns).[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136]

In his victory speech Starmer thanked Labour Party workers for their work – including nearly five years of revamping and rebranding Labour in the face of Conservative dominance – and urged them to savour the moment, but warned them of challenges ahead and pledged his government would seek "national renewal":[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138]

> We did it! You campaigned for it, you fought for it, you voted for it and now it has arrived. Change begins now. And it feels good, I have to be honest. Four-and-a-half years of work changing the party. This is what it is for – a changed Labour Party ready to serve our country, ready to restore Britain to the service of working people. And across our country people will be waking up to the news, relieved that a weight has been lifted, a burden finally removed from the shoulders of this great nation. And now we can look forward. Walk into the morning, the sunlight of hope, pale at first but getting stronger through the day, shining once again, on a country with the opportunity after 14 years to get its future back. We said we would end the chaos and we will. We said we would turn the page and we have. Today we start the next chapter, begin the work of change, the mission of national renewal and start to rebuild our country.

— Keir Starmer on 5 July 2024, following his general election victory

## Premiership (2024–2026)

Further information: [Premiership of Keir Starmer](77280609.md)

### Appointment



Starmer in [Downing Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downing_Street) delivering his first speech as prime minister, 5 July 2024

As the leader of the majority party in the House of Commons, Starmer was appointed prime minister, [First Lord of the Treasury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Lord_of_the_Treasury) and [Minister for the Civil Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_the_Civil_Service) by [King Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Charles_III) on 5 July 2024, becoming the first Labour prime minister since Gordon Brown in 2010 and the first one to win a general election since [Tony Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Blair) in [2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_United_Kingdom_general_election).[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141] He also became the first prime minister to enter office with a knighthood since [Alec Douglas-Home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alec_Douglas-Home).[^cite_note-142] Starmer and his wife, Victoria, were driven from [Buckingham Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buckingham_Palace) to [Downing Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downing_Street). Starmer stopped the car on the way back from the palace to go on a walkabout in Downing Street to meet cheering crowds.[^cite_note-seddon-143]

In his first speech as prime minister, Starmer paid tribute to his predecessor, Rishi Sunak, saying "his achievement as the first British Asian prime minister of our country should not be underestimated by anyone" and he also recognised "the dedication and hard work he brought to his leadership", but added that the people of the UK had voted for change:[^cite_note-144]

> You have given us a clear mandate, and we will use it to deliver change. To restore service and respect to politics, end the era of noisy performance, tread more lightly on your lives, and unite our country. Four nations, standing together again, facing down, as we have so often in our past, the challenges of an insecure world. Committed to a calm and patient rebuilding. So with respect and humility, I invite you all to join this government of service in the mission of national renewal. Our work is urgent and we begin it today.

Other world leaders, including [Joe Biden](145422.md) and [Justin Trudeau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Trudeau), as well as Blair and Brown, congratulated Starmer upon his appointment as prime minister.[^cite_note-145] One of his first acts was to declare the [Rwanda asylum plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rwanda_asylum_plan) "dead": the Home Secretary, [Yvette Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yvette_Cooper), would establish a [Border Security Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Security_Command) to tackle [smuggling gangs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_smuggler) which facilitate [illegal migrant crossings over the English Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Channel_migrant_crossings_\(2018%E2%80%93present\)).[^cite_note-rileysmith-146][^cite_note-whannel-147] Starmer went on a tour of the [four nations of the UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom), meeting with leaders including [John Swinney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Swinney), [Michelle O'Neill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_O'Neill), and [Vaughan Gething](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaughan_Gething).[^cite_note-148] He also met the twelve [regional mayors](598892.md) and announced the establishment of the [Council of the Nations and Regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_Nations_and_Regions).[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] On 24 July 2024 he attended his first [Prime Minister's Questions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister's_Questions) in the Commons.[^cite_note-151]

### Cabinet

Main article: [Starmer ministry](77280613.md)



Starmer convening his first [Cabinet meeting](77280613.md) on 6 July 2024

Starmer set about appointing a new [Cabinet](162790.md), which first met on 6 July, and he completed his ministerial appointments on 7 July.[^cite_note-seddon-143] Parliament was then recalled to meet on 9 July.[^cite_note-152] Among Starmer's ministerial appointments were the scientist [Patrick Vallance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrick_Vallance) as [Minister of State for Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_State_for_Science), the rehabilitation campaigner [James Timpson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Timpson) as [Minister of State for Prisons, Parole and Probation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_State_for_Prisons,_Parole_and_Probation), and the international law expert [Richard Hermer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Hermer) as [Attorney General for England and Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_for_England_and_Wales), who were created life peers to sit in the [House of Lords](13658.md).[^cite_note-153] The new government also contains a few ministers from the [New Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Labour) Blair/Brown governments, including [Hilary Benn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilary_Benn), Yvette Cooper, David Lammy, and Ed Miliband in Cabinet, and [Jacqui Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacqui_Smith) and [Douglas Alexander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Alexander) as junior ministers.[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155][^cite_note-156]



Starmer's [reshuffled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_British_cabinet_reshuffle) cabinet in October 2025

In September 2025, Starmer's deputy [Angela Rayner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Rayner) resigned over a [tax scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Rayner_tax_scandal) in what was described as a damaging blow to Starmer's leadership.[^cite_note-157] Following Rayner's resignation, Starmer conducted the [first major reshuffle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_British_cabinet_reshuffle) of his premiership.[^cite_note-158]

### Peter Mandelson

In December 2024, Starmer appointed [Peter Mandelson](216970.md) to serve as [British ambassador to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_ambassador_to_the_United_States). Starmer dismissed Mandelson in September 2025 over his association with [Jeffrey Epstein](6253522.md). In February 2026, during Prime Minister's Questions, Starmer said that Mandelson had "lied repeatedly" during vetting about [his long-term relationship with Epstein](81477249.md). Starmer said he regretted the appointment and announced that, with the King's agreement, Mandelson had been removed from the [Privy Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_Council_\(United_Kingdom\)) for bringing it into disrepute.[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160] The Mandelson scandal was significant for his premiership,[^cite_note-161] and led to the resignation of Starmer's [Chief of Staff](4476964.md) [Morgan McSweeney](75790152.md) on 8 February 2026 as well as his [Director of Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downing_Street_Director_of_Communications) [Tim Allan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Allan) the following day.[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163] Hours after Allan announced his resignation, [Scottish Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottish_Labour) leader [Anas Sarwar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anas_Sarwar) called on Starmer to quit as Prime Minister, but cabinet ministers responded by voicing their support for Starmer.[^cite_note-164]

In April 2026, it was reported that Mandelson had failed security vetting for the role of ambassador. The Foreign Office, which Starmer accused of deliberately withholding information from him, overruled the recommendation of the vetting agency and allowed Mandelson to take up the post. Starmer faced calls to resign over allegations he misled Parliament when he said "full due process" was followed during the appointment.[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166]

### Public opinion

Starmer was viewed unfavourably by the British public during his tenure as prime minister.[^cite_note-EllyattCNBC-167] Starmer's average approval rating fell from +5% after the election to −30% by January 2025 before levelling off until April 2025 when it began to decline further, reaching −46% by November that year. A poll by [Ipsos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipsos) indicates that Starmer is the most unpopular prime minister since Ipsos's records began in 1977. It found 13% of the public were satisfied with Starmer's job performance, 79% dissatisfied, giving a net approval rating of minus 66.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-keir_starmer_is-169][^cite_note-IpsosSep2025-170] Starmer's average net approval remained higher than Boris Johnson's during the [Partygate scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partygate_scandal), Jeremy Corbyn's when he resigned as Labour leader and when Liz Truss resigned as prime minister.[^cite_note-171]

Starmer's unpopularity was tied to poor results for Labour in the [2025 United Kingdom local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_United_Kingdom_local_elections), [2026 United Kingdom local elections](79139854.md) and [2026 Senedd election](68245236.md). An opinion-piece in *[Politico](9172097.md)* viewed this as a likely catalyst for a leadership challenge.[^cite_note-172]

In September 2025, *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* reported that plans to replace Starmer had begun among groups of MPs.[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174] The reports were instigated by briefings to the media from Starmer's allies, which stated that he would resist any attempted challenge to his leadership.[^cite_note-175] By November, further reports emerged that plans to replace Starmer with [Wes Streeting](17169295.md) could be enacted after the [November 2025 United Kingdom budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2025_United_Kingdom_budget).[^cite_note-176] Shortly thereafter, [Clive Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clive_Lewis_\(politician\)) became the first Labour MP to publicly call for Starmer's resignation.[^cite_note-177]



[Peter Mandelson](216970.md) and Starmer in February 2025

A [YouGov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouGov) poll for *[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times)* in February 2026 indicates that 51% of respondents thought Starmer was either more sleazy or as sleazy as [Boris Johnson](19065069.md). The poll found that 43% of participants did not think Starmer had handled the [Peter Mandelson](216970.md) scandal well; 23% thought that he had; 14% thought Starmer had shown good judgement in his handling of the affair.[^cite_note-178]

### Domestic policy

Domestically, Starmer said that his primary concerns would be economic growth, reforming the planning system, infrastructure, energy, healthcare, education, childcare, and strengthening workers' rights, as set out in Labour's 2024 election manifesto. The [2024 State Opening of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_State_Opening_of_Parliament) outlined 39 bills that Labour proposed to introduce in the months ahead, including ones to [renationalise the railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Great_Britain), to bring local bus services under local public control, to strengthen the rights of workers, to tackle [illegal immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_immigration_to_the_United_Kingdom), to reform the [House of Lords](13658.md), and to undertake a programme to speed up the delivery of "high quality infrastructure" and housing. In addition, a number of bills proposed by the previous Conservative government were also included, notably the [Tobacco and Vapes Bill](78470987.md), which featured in the 2023 King's Speech, but had been [abandoned when the election was called](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wash-up_period).[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180] [Skills England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skills_England), a body whose objective will be to reduce the need for overseas employees by improving skills training for people in England, was launched on 22 July.[^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182]

#### Economy



Starmer with Reeves, 6 July 2024

The new [chancellor of the exchequer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_exchequer), [Rachel Reeves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Reeves), accused the previous government of leaving a £21.9 billion "black hole", and announced on 29 July 2024 that certain [winter fuel payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_fuel_payments) would be scrapped for around 10 million pensioners.[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184] Following [criticism of the plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_fuel_payment_abolition_backlash), Starmer defended the scrapping of these winter fuel payments, arguing that he had to make "tough decisions to stabilise the economy".[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186] On 10 September 2024, the government benches defeated a Conservative Party motion in Parliament by a majority of 120 to block the measure.[^cite_note-187] On 9 June 2025, the government, in what was described as a 'major U-turn', announced that it would reverse most of the cuts, restoring winter fuel payments to 9 million pensioners, citing improved public finances.[^cite_note-188]

Starmer's Labour Government inherited a number of [ongoing industrial disputes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_industrial_disputes_and_strikes_\(2022%E2%80%93present\)) from the preceding [Conservative Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunak_ministry) and agreed on pay deals with [trade unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_unions_in_the_United_Kingdom) representing NHS and railway workers, ending strikes in the first few months of taking office.[^cite_note-189] In August 2024, Starmer's government agreed to increase public-sector worker pay by 5 to 7 per cent.[^cite_note-190]

On 10 October 2024 the Government introduced the [Employment Rights Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_Rights_Bill), which became an Act of Parliament in December 2025.[^cite_note-191] This includes an increase in minimum wages and a number of rights, such as immediate protection against unfair dismissal and the entitlement for employees to request flexible working arrangements unless the employer can demonstrate that such arrangements are impractical.[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193] The government's inaugural International Investment Summit was held on 14 October 2024 at the [Guildhall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guildhall) in London.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195]

The [October 2024 budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2024_United_Kingdom_budget) was presented to the [House of Commons](13828.md) by [Chancellor of the Exchequer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer) Rachel Reeves on 30 October 2024. It covered Labour's fiscal plans, with a focus on investment, healthcare, education, childcare, [sustainable energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_energy), transport, and worker's rights enrichment. The [National Minimum Wage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Minimum_Wage) is set to increase by 6.7 per cent (reaching £12.21 per hour) and a £22.6 billion increase in the day-to-day health budget was announced, with a £3.1 billion increase in the capital budget. That includes £1 billion for hospital repairs and rebuilding projects.[^cite_note-196] The government plans to allocate £5 billion for housing investment in the fiscal year 2025–26, with a focus on enhancing the availability of affordable housing. Education will receive £6.7 billion of capital investment, a 19 per cent real-terms increase. This includes £1.4 billion to rebuild more than 500 schools.[^cite_note-197]

The June 2025 [Spending Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spending_Review) was presented to the House of Commons by the Chancellor, allocating day-to-day budgets for the years between 2025/26 and 2028/29 and capital budgets for the years between 2025/26 and 2029/30. It included £14.2 billion for a new nuclear power station at [Sizewell C](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sizewell_C),[^cite_note-198] £15 billion for transport projects outside London,[^cite_note-199] and £39 billion over a decade for social and affordable housing.[^cite_note-200]

#### Immigration

One of Starmer's first acts was the cancellation of the controversial [Rwanda asylum plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rwanda_asylum_plan), describing it as "dead and buried".[^cite_note-rileysmith-146][^cite_note-201] Cooper established the [Border Security Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Security_Command) to tackle [smuggling gangs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_smuggler) which facilitate [illegal migrant crossings over the English Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Channel_migrant_crossings_\(2018%E2%80%93present\)).[^cite_note-rileysmith-146][^cite_note-whannel-147]

Following the publication in November 2024 of the record [net migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Net_migration) figure updated from the previously estimated 728,000 to 906,000 for the year to June 2023, and of the 20% drop to 728,000 for the year to the end of June 2024 as a result of rule changes by the then Conservative government, Starmer said he would introduce policies aimed at reducing immigration levels.[^cite_note-202]

In a May 2025 speech and associated [white paper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_paper) on the topic of immigration, Starmer said that the UK risked becoming an "island of strangers", and that high immigration had done "incalculable damage" to society.[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204] The white paper outlined changes aimed at reducing immigration, including training for people in the UK.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] His language was criticised by the left for its perceived echoes of [Enoch Powell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enoch_Powell)'s [Rivers of Blood speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rivers_of_Blood_speech).[^cite_note-\:1-208] In June, Starmer apologised for his "island of strangers" comment, saying that the phrase "wasn't right" and "I deeply regret using it". He said that he and his speechwriters had been unaware of the similarity with Powell's words.[^cite_note-\:1-208]

On 10 July 2025, Starmer announced that a new ["one in, one out" migrant deal with France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom%E2%80%93France_one_in,_one_out_plan) would begin within weeks.[^cite_note-209] In early September 2025, it was reported that 3,567 people had arrived on the UK's shores since the deal with France was ratified in August, however returns had not yet commenced.[^cite_note-210] On 15 September 2025, the first "one in, one out" migrant flight, which was due to fly one migrant from London to Paris on an [Air France](14998012.md) flight, was cancelled due to protests from charities and threats of legal action.[^cite_note-211] On 16 September 2025, deportation flights were cancelled again due to legal challenges and protests.[^cite_note-212] Further that day it was reported that a 25-year-old [Eritrean](17238590.md) asylum seeker who had arrived on a small boat on 12 August 2025, would not be deported as planned on 17 September, after he won his High Court bid to have the removal temporarily blocked.[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214] As part of his immigration reform, on 18 November 2025, the [2025 UK refugee plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_UK_refugee_plan) was presented to Parliament by [Shabana Mahmood](27266407.md).[^cite_note-215]

In November 2025, Starmer described the decrease in net migration to 204,000 as a "step in the right direction".[^cite_note-216] Entries for the year ending June 2025 were 898,000, departures were 693,000.[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218]

#### Healthcare

On 11 September 2024 Starmer pledged that there would be no more money for the NHS without reform. In response to the report from a nine-week review conducted by the peer and NHS surgeon [Lord Darzi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Darzi), which said that the NHS in England was in a critical condition, Starmer said the solution was reform, not money, and that there will be no more money without reform.[^cite_note-219]

In Government, Starmer reaffirmed the outgoing Conservative government's commitment of no new [HIV cases in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS_in_the_United_Kingdom) by 2030. On 10 February 2025 Starmer, alongside the singer and HIV activist [Beverley Knight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beverley_Knight) and the [Terrence Higgins Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrence_Higgins_Trust) chief executive Richard Angell, recorded himself taking a [rapid HIV home test](339553.md#Rapid_or_point-of-care_tests). This made Starmer the first serving British prime minister and serving [G7](1196634.md) leader to take a test on camera.[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221] In March 2025 Starmer, along with [Health Secretary](356531.md) [Wes Streeting](17169295.md) announced a two-year plan to abolish [NHS England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHS_England), saying it would to reduce bureaucracy and increase funding available for more effective purposes within the service.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223]

#### Welfare and pensions

Starmer initially declined to abolish the [two-child benefit cap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-child_benefit_cap) introduced by the [Cameron–Clegg coalition government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameron%E2%80%93Clegg_coalition_government) in 2013, citing financial reasons.[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226] On 23 July 2024 Labour [withdrew the whip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrew_the_whip) from seven of its MPs who had supported an amendment tabled by the [Scottish National Party](28887.md)'s Westminster parliamentary leader [Stephen Flynn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Flynn) to scrap it, with Flynn saying that scrapping the cap would immediately raise 300,000 children out of poverty. MPs rejected the SNP amendment by 363 votes to 103.[^cite_note-227] The seven Labour MPs [suspended](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_discipline) for six months were [John McDonnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McDonnell), [Richard Burgon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Burgon), [Ian Byrne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Byrne), [Apsana Begum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apsana_Begum), [Imran Hussain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imran_Hussain_\(British_politician\)), [Zarah Sultana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zarah_Sultana), and [Rebecca Long-Bailey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebecca_Long-Bailey), all of whom sat as [independents](2172378.md); all MPs, (with the exception of Sultana, who resigned from the party in July 2025 to co-found [Your Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Your_Party_\(UK\)) with former Labour Leader [Jeremy Corbyn](415036.md)), were later readmitted to the Parliamentary Labour Party.[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229] A Child Poverty Taskforce was launched by Starmer, in which expert officials from across government would work together on how best to support more than four million children living in poverty.[^cite_note-230] Starmer eventually u-turned in November 2025, abolishing the two-child benefit cap in [that month's fiscal budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2025_United_Kingdom_budget).[^cite_note-231]

In July 2025 the government's Universal Credit Bill passed the House of Commons. It will increase the standard rate of [Universal Credit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Credit).[^cite_note-232] The measures reduce the health-related aspect of universal credit for certain claimants, but they make sure that other parts of the benefit increase beyond inflation.[^cite_note-233]

#### Education

Starmer's government has imposed VAT on private school fees, expanded free school meals to 500,000 more children,[^cite_note-234] and opened 750 free breakfast clubs in primary schools.[^cite_note-235] It also presided over an increase in tuition fees for higher education in England along with an increase in [maintenance loans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_loan).[^cite_note-236]

#### Criminal justice

Shortly after taking office, Starmer said that there were "too many prisoners",[^cite_note-237] and described the previous government as having acted "almost beyond recklessness".[^cite_note-238] Saying it would help manage prison overcrowding, the newly appointed [Justice Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Justice), [Shabana Mahmood](27266407.md), announced the implementation of an early release scheme which allowed for prisoners in England and Wales to be released after serving 40 per cent of their sentences rather than the 50 per cent previously introduced under the last government.[^cite_note-239] Over 1,700 prisoners were released in September, with further releases expected in the following year.[^cite_note-240] It then emerged that one prisoner released early under the scheme was charged with sexual assault relating to an alleged offence against a woman on the same day he was freed.[^cite_note-241] Starmer has defended the releasing of prisoners, and accused the previous government of having "broke\[n] the prison system", with plans to build new prisons.[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243]

Critics have accused Starmer's government of [two-tier policing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-tier_policing), which has led to these critics labelling him with the moniker "Two-Tier Keir".[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245] Allegations of two-tier policing were prevalent during the [Southport riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southport_riots) in August 2024 and the subsequent arrest of rioters and some of their supporters, such as [Lucy Connolly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucy_Connolly), along with the Epping protests in July 2025.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247] Conversely, critics such as [Jamie Driscoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamie_Driscoll) believe that the police are more favourable to right-wing protests and more heavy-handed against left-wing protests and groups such as [Palestine Action](67777543.md), which was proscribed by Starmer's government in 2025.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249]

#### 2024 England and Northern Ireland riots



Starmer receives senior [British police officers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_ranks_of_the_United_Kingdom) at 10 Downing Street, 1 August 2024.

Following the [2024 Southport stabbing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Southport_stabbing), in which three young girls were killed, Starmer described the incident as horrendous and shocking and thanked [emergency services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_services) for their swift response.[^cite_note-250] He visited Southport and laid flowers at the scene, where he was heckled by some members of the public.[^cite_note-Telegraph_heckled2-251] Starmer later wrote amidst [the riots across England and Northern Ireland following the stabbing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_Kingdom_riots) that those who had "hijacked the vigil for the victims" had "insulted the community as it grieves" and that rioters would feel the full force of the law.[^cite_note-252]

On 1 August, and following a meeting with senior police officers, Starmer announced the establishment of a National Violent Disorder Programme to facilitate greater cooperation between police forces when dealing with [violent disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_disorder).[^cite_note-253] On 4 August Starmer stated that rioters "will feel the full force of the law" and that "You will regret taking part in this, whether directly or those whipping up this action online and then running away themselves". He added "I won't shy away from calling it what it is – far-right thuggery".[^cite_note-Whitehead-2024-254][^cite_note-255] Starmer later called an emergency response meeting of [COBRA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_Office_Briefing_Rooms).[^cite_note-256] After the COBRA meeting, Starmer ordered a "standing army" to be set up to tackle the ongoing "far-right" riots. This was possible under the special emergency powers which were first used 40 years ago under the [Ridley Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ridley_Plan), to tackle striking miners in 1984 and 1985.[^cite_note-257]

Following the 2024 UK riots, the Starmer government announced that it is reviewing the [Online Safety Act 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Online_Safety_Act_2023) with the possibility of strengthening its provisions.[^cite_note-258]

Starmer rejected calls from some MPs – including the Labour MP [Diane Abbott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diane_Abbott), the [Reform UK](59757202.md) leader [Nigel Farage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigel_Farage) and the Conservative MP [Dame Priti Patel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dame_Priti_Patel) – to [recall Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recall_of_parliament) to [Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Westminster).[^cite_note-259] After he said "large social media companies and those who run them" were contributing to the disorder, [Elon Musk](909036.md), the owner of the social media website [X](9988187.md), criticised Starmer for not condemning all participants and only blaming the [far-right](221220.md).[^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261] Musk further said Starmer was not protecting all communities in the United Kingdom, which he said had a "two-tier" policing system.[^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264]

#### Acceptance of gifts

In September 2024, Starmer and fellow [senior government ministers](739237.md) faced criticism for accepting gifts from [Labour](19279158.md) [donors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_party_funding).[^cite_note-265] Starmer also faced [accusations of breaking parliamentary rules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Labour_Party_freebies_controversy) by not declaring £5,000 worth of clothes bought for [his wife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_Starmer) by the Labour donor [Lord Alli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Alli).[^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267] That same month, [Sky News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sky_News) reported that Starmer had received £107,145 in gifts, benefits, and hospitality since December 2019, which was two-and-a-half times more than any other MP.[^cite_note-268]

### Defence



Starmer visiting a [Vanguard-class submarine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanguard-class_submarine) which provide [Britain's nuclear deterrent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trident_\(UK_nuclear_programme\))

On 25 February 2025, Starmer announced that the previous government's commitment to reaching 2.5% of GDP spending on defence by 2030 would be brought forward to April 2027. This would be funded by reducing the [official development assistance](1316305.md) budget from 0.5% to 0.3%.[^cite_note-govuk-20250225-269][^cite_note-fullfact-20260115-270] [George Robertson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Robertson,_Baron_Robertson_of_Port_Ellen), a former [Secretary General of NATO](22194433.md) and Labour politician, led the [2025 Strategic Defence Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Defence_Review_\(2025\)) to guide the future increased spending, with Starmer commenting that the UK was moving to "war-fighting readiness" and that "everything we do will add to the strength of [NATO](21133.md)".[^cite_note-ph-20250602-271]

In June 2025, Starmer announced the purchase of [F-35A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-35A) aircraft capable of deploying US nuclear weapons as part of NATO's [dual capable aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual_capable_aircraft) nuclear mission, which his office said would be "the biggest strengthening of the [UK's nuclear posture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_weapons_of_the_United_Kingdom) in a generation".[^cite_note-bbc-20250624-272][^cite_note-govuk-20250624-273]

The Defence Investment Plan to implement the review was expected to be issued in autumn 2025. However this was delayed amid warnings that there was a £28 billion funding gap for shortfalls on previous commitments.[^cite_note-guardian-20260205-274][^cite_note-times-20260604-275] The [Public Accounts Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Accounts_Committee_\(United_Kingdom\)) reported that the delay was undermining the UK's credibility with NATO allies and confidence within the [defence industrial base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_industrial_base).[^cite_note-guardian-20260607-276] On 11 June 2026, just before the planned [Defence Investment Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Defence_Investment_Plan) publication, Defence Secretary [John Healey](434625.md) and his junior minister [Al Carns](77290491.md) resigned citing disagreement with the very limited spending increase given in the plan.[^cite_note-guardian-20260611-277]

The Defence Investment Plan was announced by Starmer on 30 June 2026, after he had announced his [resignation as prime minister and Leader of the Labour Party](83157483.md#Starmer's_resignation).[^cite_note-278] The plan increased spending by about £1.5 billion over the previous draft, though funding for all the spending was not initially allocated.[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280]

### Foreign policy

Main article: [List of international prime ministerial trips made by Keir Starmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_international_prime_ministerial_trips_made_by_Keir_Starmer)

*Politico* reported that Starmer divided his time half and half between foreign and domestic matters, and in his first 17 months as prime minister visited 44 countries on 37 trips from the UK.[^cite_note-politico-20260213-281] Starmer said it would not be possible for a Prime Minister to spend less time on foreign matters in current circumstances.[^cite_note-guardian-20260703-282]

#### United States



Starmer with US president Joe Biden in the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office), 10 July 2024



Starmer with US president Donald Trump in the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office), 27 February 2025

In July 2024, following the 2024 general election, US president [Joe Biden](145422.md) congratulated Starmer on "a hell of a victory".[^cite_note-283] Starmer and Biden discussed their shared commitment to the [Special Relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Relationship) between the US and the UK, as well as their mutual support of Ukraine.
