Following the [attempted assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Donald_Trump_in_Pennsylvania) of [Donald Trump](4848272.md) in July 2024, the former [president of the United States](24113.md) at the time, Starmer posted on Twitter saying "Political violence in any form has no place in our societies" and extended his best wishes to Trump and [his family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_of_Donald_Trump).[^cite_note-284]

In September 2024, during a visit to [New York City](645042.md) to address the [UN General Assembly](31957.md), Starmer met the American [Republican Party](32070.md) presidential candidate, Trump, at [Trump Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_Tower). Following the meeting, Starmer said it was "good" to have met with Trump and that the meeting was an opportunity for both Trump and Starmer to establish a working relationship.[^cite_note-285] Following Trump's election victory in the [2024 United States presidential election](59568546.md), Starmer called Trump to formally congratulate him on 6 November and was assured that the "special relationship" between the United Kingdom and United States "would continue to thrive".[^cite_note-286]

In February 2025, Starmer met with President Trump at the [White House](33057.md) to discuss continued support to Ukraine and a potential peace deal. They additionally discussed a potential trade deal. He also presented a handwritten letter from [King Charles III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Charles_III) inviting the President to a historic second state visit to the UK.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288]

In June 2025 Starmer supported Israel in the [Twelve-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve-Day_War) and [United States strikes on Iranian nuclear sites](80262844.md), stating that "Iran can never be allowed to develop a nuclear weapon and the US has taken action to alleviate that threat".[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290]

#### NATO



Starmer and Secretary-General [Jens Stoltenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jens_Stoltenberg) at the NATO summit in 2024

The first overseas meeting Starmer attended as prime minister was the [2024 NATO summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_NATO_summit) held in [Washington](108956.md) from 9 to 11 July 2024.[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292] On the flight to the summit, Starmer laid out a "cast iron" commitment to increase [defence spending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_spending) to the NATO target of 2.5 per cent of [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_United_Kingdom) in line with the NATO target, following a "root and branch" review of [British armed forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_armed_forces).[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294]

#### Europe

Since he became prime minister, Starmer has sought to "reset" [UK relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Brexit_United_Kingdom_relations_with_the_European_Union) with the [European Union](9317.md) following [Brexit](41688778.md), which he opposed. He met with a number of European leaders during his first few months in office.[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296]

On 27 August 2024, Starmer and German Chancellor [Olaf Scholz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olaf_Scholz) announced talks to revise a [Germany](11867.md)-[UK](31717.md) [co-operation agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partnership_and_Cooperation_Agreement) covering areas including defence, energy security, and science and technology.[^cite_note-297]

#### Russo-Ukrainian war

Further information: [United Kingdom and the Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_and_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine)



Starmer with Ukrainian president [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md), 10 July 2024

At the 2024 NATO summit, Starmer signalled that [Ukraine](31750.md) could use British [Storm Shadow](306908.md) [missiles](19594.md), [sent by HMG by way of military aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_military_aid_to_Ukraine_during_the_Russo-Ukrainian_War), to strike military targets inside [Russia](25391.md), during the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-298] In a meeting with Ukrainian president [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md) Starmer called for an "irreversible" [membership strategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine%E2%80%93NATO_relations) for Ukraine to [join NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlargement_of_NATO).[^cite_note-299]

Following a [heated meeting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Trump-Zelenskyy_meeting) between US President Trump and President Zelenskyy at the White House, Starmer organised a [summit of European leaders in London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_London_Summit_on_Ukraine). Among the things discussed at the summit were continued support for Ukraine, security guarantees for the country and peace efforts.[^cite_note-300]

#### China



Starmer with Chinese president [Xi Jinping](2017814.md), 30 January 2026

In November 2024, Starmer met Chinese president [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) at the [G20 summit in Rio de Janeiro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_G20_Rio_de_Janeiro_summit) and told him he wanted to build a 'consistent, durable, respectful' relationship with China.[^cite_note-301] In January 2026, Starmer approved Chinese government plans for a new embassy in London.[^cite_note-302] In the same month, he [visited China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_visit_by_Keir_Starmer_to_China), becoming the first British prime minister to visit China since 2018.[^cite_note-303] Starmer's trip primarily focused on broadening trade ties, bringing a delegation heavy on [British banking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_banking) executives, including [HSBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HSBC), [Barclays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barclays), and [Standard Chartered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Chartered), and cultural emissaries representing the arts and sports, as well as manufacturers, including [Airbus](26220236.md), [AstraZeneca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AstraZeneca), [Brompton Bikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brompton_Bicycle), [Jaguar Land Rover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaguar_Land_Rover), and [McLaren Automotive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McLaren_Automotive).[^cite_note-304]

A "China spy case" that came to light in 2024 involving Christopher Cash, a former parliamentary researcher, and Christopher Berry collapsed in September 2025 after the [Crown Prosecution Service](383064.md) (CPS) dropped charges under the [Official Secrets Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_Secrets_Act). The two were accused of passing information harmful to UK interests between December 2021 and February 2023. Director of Public Prosecutions [Stephen Parkinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Parkinson_\(lawyer\)) said the case fell apart because the government failed to provide evidence that China was officially considered a national security threat at the time, as required under a 2025 legal precedent. Conservative leader [Kemi Badenoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemi_Badenoch) accused Starmer's government of withholding key evidence to appease China, while the government denied interference and blamed outdated laws and the previous administration's stance on China.[^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307]

#### Gaza war

Further information: [United Kingdom and the Gaza war](76740255.md)

On the [Gaza war](74998519.md), Starmer has supported Israel's right to self-defence and has not blocked all arms sales to Israel. He has also condemned some of Israel's actions, called for a ceasefire, committed humanitarian aid to Gaza, and supported a [two-state solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-state_solution).[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310]

In October 2023, shortly after the [Hamas attack on Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamas_attack_on_Israel), when asked what a proportionate response by Israel would be, Starmer stated that Israel had the "right to defend herself" and "Israel has the right to do everything it can" to return the hostages. When subsequently asked whether a siege involving the cutting off of food and water was appropriate, he responded, "I think Israel does have that right" adding that "everything should be done within international law but I don't want to step away from the sort of core principles that Israel has a right to defend herself and Hamas bears responsibility for these terrorist acts".[^cite_note-lbc-2025-102] This sparked significant controversy within his party, and several Labour councillors resigned in protest. Starmer then said that he was referring to the right to self-defence, "I was not saying that Israel had the right to cut off water, food, fuel or medicines".[^cite_note-sky-2023-104]

When he became prime minister, Starmer told Israeli Prime Minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md) of the "urgent need for a ceasefire, the return of hostages and an immediate increase in the volume of humanitarian aid". He also assured Netanyahu that the UK would continue its "vital cooperation to deter malign threats" with Israel.[^cite_note-311]

Under Starmer's premiership, the licences of some [British arms sales to Israel](76740255.md#Military_exports) were suspended in September 2024 because of a "clear risk" that the weapons could be used to violate international law. [Foreign Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Secretary_\(United_Kingdom\)) David Lammy announced the UK Government's suspension of 30 of 350 arms export licences to Israel, affecting equipment such as parts for fighter jets, helicopters and drones.[^cite_note-312] However, in the last three months of 2024, UK arms licenses to Israel totalled £127.6 million, exceeding the £115 million worth of military equipment sent to Israel in the prior four years, 2020 to 2023.[^cite_note-313]



Keir Starmer with Israeli president [Isaac Herzog](4645588.md) at 10 Downing Street, 10 September 2025

Starmer's government stated in November 2024 that Netanyahu would be arrested if he were to travel to the UK, after the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) issued [an international arrest warrant for him](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Criminal_Court_arrest_warrants_for_Israeli_leaders) over alleged war crimes.[^cite_note-314]

In May 2025, Starmer issued a joint statement with [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md) and [Mark Carney](13571049.md) condemning Israel's [renewed offensive against Gaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2025_Gaza_offensive). He called for Israel to immediately stop its military operations and to immediately allow humanitarian aid into Gaza. The statement condemned remarks by members of the Israeli government suggesting the destruction in Gaza would lead to relocation of its population as "abhorrent" and against international law. He said his government would take "concrete actions" if Israel continued its "egregious actions".[^cite_note-315] Netanyahu accused Starmer of siding with Hamas, saying "you're on the wrong side of justice, you're on the wrong side of humanity and you're on the wrong side of history".[^cite_note-316]

On 5 July, the government made the protest group [Palestine Action](67777543.md) a "[proscribed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proscribed) organisation" under the [Terrorism Act 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_Act_2000), following its vandalism of military aircraft at [RAF Brize Norton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAF_Brize_Norton), alleged to support the Israeli military.[^cite_note-317] On 6 August, at a Labour [National Executive Committee](343817.md) meeting, Starmer said the group had targeted Jewish-owned businesses, and that the ban was not meant "to stifle debate on Palestine".[^cite_note-318] On 9 August, the [Metropolitan Police](192450.md) made 532 arrests, its most in at least a decade, at a protest in [Parliament Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_Square). Of these, 522 were for "displaying an item in support of a proscribed organisation", as most protestors carried placards reading "I oppose genocide. I support Palestine Action." [Amnesty International](18947898.md) and [Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberty_\(advocacy_group\)) condemned the proscription as infringing on [freedom of speech](21401843.md).[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320] Protests against the banning of Palestine Action continued into September 2025, with 890 arrests in one weekend.[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322]



Starmer at the [Gaza peace summit](81324529.md) in Egypt, 13 October 2025

On 29 July 2025, Starmer announced that the United Kingdom would conditionally [recognise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_recognition_of_Palestine) the [State of Palestine](241405.md) at the September opening of the [United Nations General Assembly](31957.md), contingent on Israel's noncompliance with a ceasefire, the facilitation of humanitarian aid, and a commitment to a two-state solution. The announcement followed a letter signed by 225 MPs, including over half of Labour, calling for immediate recognition. One of its coordinators, [Sarah Champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Champion), criticised the conditionality, while Netanyahu and Conservative politicians condemned the move as rewarding Hamas.[^cite_note-323] On 21 September, Starmer announced that the United Kingdom formally recognised Palestine as an independent state.[^cite_note-324]

#### Sudan

In late 2025, Starmer's government faced mounting pressure to suspend [arms sales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_arms_export) to the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) following reports that British-made military equipment was being diverted to the [Rapid Support Forces](43053874.md) (RSF) in [Sudan](27421.md), which had been accused of committing [war crimes](74944426.md) and [genocide](75311533.md) during the ongoing [Sudanese civil war](73556699.md).[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326]

### 2026 leadership crisis and resignation

See also: [2026 Labour Party leadership crisis](83157483.md)

Approval ratings of Starmer as prime minister



Starmer leaving Number 10 Downing Street on his last day as prime minister.



Starmer delivers his resignation speech at 10 Downing Street.

In May 2026, Starmer faced a political crisis following reports of internal divisions within the [Labour government](77280613.md) and speculation over his future leadership, following on from poor popularity ratings in opinion polls from soon after he became prime minister.[^cite_note-EllyattCNBC-167][^cite_note-keir_starmer_is-169] The crisis intensified after the resignation of several senior ministers and growing criticism from factions within the Labour Party concerning [economic policy](210522.md), immigration, and electoral strategy. Media outlets reported discussions among Labour MPs regarding potential leadership alternatives, including senior cabinet figures and regional mayors.[^cite_note-327] During a cabinet meeting, Starmer rejected calls to resign and challenged critics within the party to formally contest the leadership if they wished to replace him. Under Labour Party rules, a leadership challenge would require the support of 20 per cent of [Labour MPs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_MPs).[^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330]

On 22 June 2026, Starmer announced his intention to resign as prime minister and Leader of the Labour Party and asked the [party's National Executive Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_Party_National_Executive_Committee) to set the timetable for choosing his successor, with nominations due to open on 9 July and the process to conclude before the summer recess. Starmer is to remain in his positions until the conclusion of [the leadership election](83143566.md).[^cite_note-starmerresignation-331]

On 20 July 2026 Starmer formally tendered his resignation to [Charles III](125248.md) at [Buckingham Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buckingham_Palace) and was officially succeeded by Burnham hours later.[^cite_note-332]

## Post-premiership (2026–present)

Upon leaving office as prime minister, Starmer's spokesperson said he intended to remain an MP after he had formally resigned his premiership, but was likely to move to the backbench rather than accept a post in Burnham's cabinet.[^cite_note-333] However, hours before the parliament returned from summer recess on 1 September, Starmer announced that he is standing down as an MP to focus on a role in international affairs,[^cite_note-334] and left the Commons later the same day upon his appointment to the [Chiltern Hundreds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiltern_Hundreds), triggering the [2026 Holborn and St Pancras by-election](84114410.md).[^cite_note-335] It was also announced Starmer would be giving out [Resignation Honours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister's_Resignation_Honours) despite previously saying he wouldn't.[^cite_note-336]

## Political positions

Main article: [Political positions of Keir Starmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_Keir_Starmer)

Starmer's political positions changed significantly after the [2020 Labour Party leadership election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Labour_Party_leadership_election_\(UK\)), which he won on a ten-pledge, left-leaning platform. Most of the pledges, including increasing [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax) on the top 5 per cent of earners, abolishing [university tuition fees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_tuition_fees), and support for [freedom of movement](1270497.md), were abandoned or substantially changed during Starmer's tenure as Labour and Opposition leader. Starmer defended changing positions on these issues, stating that shifting economic circumstances made these pledges unrealistic.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340]

Some commentators, judging that Starmer has led his party towards the political centre in order to improve its electability, attempt to liken what he has accomplished in this regard with Tony Blair's development of [New Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Labour).[^cite_note-Webb_20232-341] Others regard his changes of policy as testament that Starmer holds no clearly defined philosophy.[^cite_note-342][^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344] A third group think that Starmer does subscribe to a definite ideology and that it is towards the left end of the socialist spectrum, arguing that "Labour under Starmer has advanced a politics of [anti-neoliberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-neoliberalism) like that of Jeremy Corbyn and [John McDonnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McDonnell)", and that Starmer "differs markedly from New Labour" in "aspiring to restructure [an economic model perceived to have failed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberalism)".[^cite_note-Webb_20232-341]

Figures including Starmer's former boss – the barrister [Geoffrey Robertson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Robertson)[^cite_note-345] – his former advisor [Simon Fletcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Fletcher_\(political_advisor\)),[^cite_note-346] and the journalist and broadcaster [Peter Oborne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Oborne),[^cite_note-347] have described Starmer as exhibiting an [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian) approach.[^cite_note-348] In office, he initiated plans to garner support from authoritarian-leaning voters.[^cite_note-349]

Despite the lack of consensus as yet about the character and even existence of Starmer's ideology, it has acquired a neologism, Starmerism, and his supporters have been called Starmerites.[^cite_note-\:13-350][^cite_note-\:322-351] Starmer's advisor [Morgan McSweeney](75790152.md) is often credited with having significantly influenced Starmer's political positions.[^cite_note-352][^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355]



Starmer and Bridget Phillipson, [Secretary of State for Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Education), visit primary school children in [Orpington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orpington).

In April 2023 Starmer gave an interview to *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* on defining Starmerism.[^cite_note-\:322-351][^cite_note-\:422-356] In this interview, two main strands of Starmerism were identified.[^cite_note-\:422-356] The first strand focused on a critique of the British state for being too ineffective and over-centralised. The answer to this critique was to base governance on five main missions to be followed over two terms of government: these missions would determine all government policy. The second strand was the adherence to an economic policy of "[modern supply-side economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Securonomics)" based on expanding economic productivity by increasing participation in the [labour market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_market), reducing inequality, expanding skills, mitigating the impact of [Brexit](41688778.md) and simplifying the [construction planning process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Construction_place).[^cite_note-\:422-356]

In June 2023, Starmer gave an interview to [*Time*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\)) where he was asked to define Starmerism, stating: "Recognising that our economy needs to be fixed. Recognising that \[solving] climate change isn't just an obligation; it's the single biggest opportunity that we've got for our country going forward. Recognizing that public services need to be reformed, that every child and every place should have the best opportunities and that we need a safe environment, safe streets, *et cetera*."[^cite_note-auto2-357]

Starmer is a member of the [Fabian Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabian_Society).[^cite_note-358][^cite_note-359] He supports [social ownership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_ownership) and investment in the UK's public services.[^cite_note-10_Pledges2-360][^cite_note-New_European2-67][^cite_note-361] He has remained committed to [renationalising the railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_British_Railways) and local bus services, the creation of a publicly owned energy company, and stricter regulation of water and energy companies.[^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365] He advocates for reducing the [voting age to 16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_age), a change that is set to be implemented in the UK by [the next general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Next_United_Kingdom_general_election).[^cite_note-366]



Starmer visits Trydan Gwyrdd Cymru Wind Farm with [First Minister of Wales](602076.md), Baroness Eluned Morgan in Carmarthenshire, to promote [Great British Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_British_Energy).

Starmer has repeatedly emphasised the reform of public institutions (against a [tax and spend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_and_spend) approach), [localism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Localism_\(politics\)), and [devolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution_in_the_United_Kingdom). He has pledged to [abolish the House of Lords](3371414.md), which he describes as "indefensible", during the first term of a Labour government and to replace it with a directly elected Assembly of the Regions and Nations, the details of which will be subject to scrutiny by [public consultation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_consultation). He criticised the Conservatives for creating [peerages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peerage) for "cronies and donors".[^cite_note-367] Starmer tasked the former Labour prime minister Gordon Brown with recommending [British constitutional reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_reform_in_the_United_Kingdom),[^cite_note-368] whose report was published in 2022. Endorsed and promoted by Starmer, Brown's report recommended the abolition of the House of Lords, extending greater powers to local councils and mayors, and deeper [devolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution) to the [countries of the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-369] Labour's 2024 election manifesto committed to the removal of the [remaining hereditary peers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remaining_hereditary_peers) from the [chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_House), setting a [mandatory retirement age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_retirement_age) of [80](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ageing), and beginning a consultation on replacing the Lords with a "more representative" body.[^cite_note-370] A 2025 pre-conference *[Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* editorial stated Starmer generally took an Anglo-American not European position, and had referenced the American centrist *[Abundance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abundance_\(Klein_and_Thompson_book\))* book and buzzword for developing prosperity by deregulation, rapid infrastructure development and market-led growth.[^cite_note-guardian-20250926-371]

Starmer strongly favours [green policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_politics) to tackle [climate change](5042951.md) and [decarbonise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decarbonise) the British economy. He has committed to eliminate [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel) from the [UK electricity grid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Grid_\(Great_Britain\)) by 2030.[^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373]

Starmer has supported policies to improve animal welfare.[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375] In December 2025 and early 2026, Starmer's [Defra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defra) announced a large suite of animal welfare changes, including bans on hen cages, pig [farrowing crates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farrowing_crates), and [low-welfare chicken breeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Broiler#Welfare_issues) on farms.[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378]

In a July 2024 statement to *[PinkNews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PinkNews)* ahead of the 2024 election, Starmer stated the Labour Party supported [LGBT rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_the_United_Kingdom), including strengthening protections against hate crimes targeting members of the [LGBT community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_community), "modernising" the "intrusive and outdated" gender recognition framework, and a proposed, "trans-inclusive" ban on [conversion therapy](26410.md).[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-meighan-380] After taking office, the Labour Party reiterated plans for the ban as part of the King's speech.[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382][^cite_note-383]

The government continued to block the [Gender Recognition Reform Bill in Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_Recognition_Reform_\(Scotland\)_Bill),[^cite_note-384][^cite_note-meighan-380][^cite_note-385] while Starmer personally has ruled out allowing [transgender people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender_people) to [self-identify](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_self-identification), and has stated that [trans women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans_women) should not have the right to use [women-only spaces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women-only_space).[^cite_note-386] Following the Supreme Court decision of *[For Women Scotland Ltd v The Scottish Ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/For_Women_Scotland_Ltd_v_The_Scottish_Ministers)* on 16 April 2025, Starmer, through a spokesperson, stated that he no longer believed that transgender women could be considered women, in accordance with the Court's ruling that the definition of a woman in the [Equality Act 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_Act_2010) only constituted "biological women".[^cite_note-387]

After the [murder of George Floyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_George_Floyd) by the police officer [Derek Chauvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derek_Chauvin) in the United States, against which numerous protests were held in the [Western world](21208200.md), Starmer supported the [Black Lives Matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Lives_Matter) [movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_movement), and [took the knee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Took_the_knee) alongside his deputy, Angela Rayner.[^cite_note-388] One year on from Floyd's murder, Starmer promised a Race Equality Act, which he said would be a "defining cause" for his Labour Government.[^cite_note-389]

In the wake of the [murder of Sarah Everard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_Sarah_Everard) in March 2021, Starmer called for longer sentences for rape and sexual violence.[^cite_note-390] Starmer said he wanted to reduce crime, maintaining that "too many people do not feel safe in their streets".[^cite_note-BBC_News-2021-391] He has pledged to halve the rates of violence against women and girls, halve the rates of serious violent crime, halve the incidents of knife crime, increase confidence in the criminal justice system, and create a 'Charging Commission'[^cite_note-392] which would be "tasked with coming up with reforms to reverse the decline in the number of offences being solved".[^cite_note-393] He has also committed to placing specialist domestic violence workers in the [control rooms](2736110.md) of every police force responding to 999 calls to support victims of abuse.[^cite_note-394] In June 2024 Starmer pledged to reduce the record high level of [legal immigration to the UK](1247786.md),[^cite_note-395] and aims to reduce net migration by improving training and skills for British workers.[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397]

In December 2023, Starmer used [Margaret Thatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher), as well as Tony Blair and [Clement Attlee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clement_Attlee), as examples of how politicians can effect "meaningful change" by acting "in service of the British people, rather than dictating to them".[^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399] Starmer has described the Labour Party as "deeply patriotic" and credits its most successful leaders, Attlee, [Harold Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Wilson), and Blair, for policies "rooted in the everyday concerns of working people".[^cite_note-BBC_News-2021-391] In a speech in May 2023, Starmer stated:[^cite_note-400]

> Don't mistake me, the very best of progressive politics is found in our determination to push Britain forward. A hunger, an ambition, that we can seize the opportunities of tomorrow and make them work for working people. But this ambition must never become unmoored from working people's need for stability, for order, security. The Conservative Party can no longer claim to be conservative. It conserves nothing we value — not our rivers and seas, not our NHS or BBC, not our families, not our nation. We must understand that there are precious things – in our way of life, in our environment, in our communities – that it is our responsibility to protect, preserve, and pass on to future generations. If that sounds Conservative, then let me tell you: I don't care.

— Keir Starmer

In June 2026, Starmer announced that the government intended to introduce [primary legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_legislation) by 25 December 2026 to restrict the use of selected [social media](5897742.md) platforms that enable "user to user" messaging and linked algorithms, with live streaming. The government restrictions are intended to be imposed by early 2027 for under-16s.[^cite_note-401]

## Personal life



Starmer and his wife, Victoria, outside 10 Downing Street, 5 July 2024

Starmer met [Victoria Alexander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_Starmer), then a solicitor, in the early 2000s. At the time he was a senior barrister with [Doughty Street Chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doughty_Street_Chambers), and they were working on the same case. They became engaged in 2004 and married on 6 May 2007 at the Fennes Estate near [Bocking, Essex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bocking,_Essex).[^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403] The couple have two children: a son, who was born a year after their wedding, and a daughter, born two years later. Prior to moving into Downing Street, the family lived in a [townhouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Townhouse) in [Kentish Town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kentish_Town), north-west London.[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406] In 2026 two foreign nationals were convicted at the [Old Bailey](229046.md) of conspiring to carry out arson on the property and on a vehicle connected to the Starmer family.[^cite_note-407] The attack was allegedly orchestrated by a Russian-based network as part of a wider campaign of sabotage, vandalism and disinformation across Europe.[^cite_note-408]

Starmer is a [pescatarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pescatarian), and his wife is a vegetarian. They raised their children as vegetarians until they were 10 years old, at which point they were given the option of eating meat.[^cite_note-409] In an interview during the 2024 general election campaign, Starmer said that his biggest fear about becoming prime minister was how it might impact his children, due to their "difficult ages" and how it would be easier if they were younger or older.[^cite_note-410] During the 2024 general election campaign, Starmer said in an interview that he would try to avoid working on Friday evenings in order to observe [Shabbat dinners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shabbat_dinner) and spend time with his family.[^cite_note-Gillott-2023-411][^cite_note-412]

Starmer is an atheist and chose to take a "solemn affirmation" (rather than an [oath](273418.md)) of [allegiance to the monarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oath_of_Allegiance_\(United_Kingdom\)) in 2022.[^cite_note-413] He has said that although he does not believe in God, he recognises the power of faith to bring people together and has been supportive of raising his children in [Judaism](15624.md).[^cite_note-414][^cite_note-415][^cite_note-416] He regularly accompanies his family to services at the [Liberal Jewish Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Jewish_Synagogue) in North London.[^cite_note-Gillott-2023-411][^cite_note-417] Starmer is a keen footballer, having played for Homerton Academicals, a north London amateur team.[^cite_note-Moss-2009-13] He is a long-standing supporter of the [Premier League](11250.md) football club [Arsenal](2174.md), holding a season-ticket at the [Emirates Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirates_Stadium). He takes his seat on match-days as a guest in the club's directors' box, which he says is due to security concerns as prime minister.[^cite_note-Maguire-2020-7][^cite_note-418]

From September 2024 until July 2026, Starmer and his family kept a [Siberian cat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siberian_cat), named Prince, at 10 Downing Street.[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420]

On 26 December 2024, Starmer's brother Nick died following a cancer diagnosis. Starmer issued a statement the next day paying tribute to him.[^cite_note-421]

## Awards and honours

Starmer [took silk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Took_silk) in 2002, when he was appointed [Queen's Counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen's_Counsel) (now [King's Counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King's_Counsel)).[^cite_note-422] In 2005, he received the [Bar Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Council_of_the_Bar)'s Sydney Elland Goldsmith Award[^cite_note-423] for his contribution to *[pro bono](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro_bono)* legal work challenging the death penalty in Uganda, Kenya, Malawi and the Caribbean.[^cite_note-424]

For services to law and criminal justice, Starmer was appointed a [Knight Commander of the Order of the Bath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knight_Commander_of_the_Order_of_the_Bath) (KCB) in the [2014 New Year Honours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_New_Year_Honours).[^cite_note-The_London_Gazette-2013-50][^cite_note-425] The appointment entitled him to wear the Order's badge and star on appropriate occasions.[^cite_note-426]

Starmer was elected an Honorary Fellow of [St Edmund Hall, Oxford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Edmund_Hall,_Oxford), in 2022.[^cite_note-427] In 2025, *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))* included him in the [Time 100](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_100), its annual list of the world's 100 most influential people.[^cite_note-428]

On 13 July 2026, French president [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md) appointed Starmer a [Grand Officier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legion_of_Honour#Classes_and_insignia) of the [Légion d'honneur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A9gion_d'honneur), in recognition of his contribution to European security and his role in establishing the "[coalition of the willing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_of_the_willing_\(Russo-Ukrainian_war\))" in support of Ukraine.[^cite_note-429] Three days later, Ukrainian president [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md) awarded him the [Order of Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Liberty_\(Ukraine\)), Ukraine's highest state decoration that may be conferred on a foreign national, in recognition of his support for Ukraine and its defence against the Russian invasion.[^cite_note-430]

### Privy Council

Starmer was sworn in as a member of the [Privy Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom) on 19 July 2017,[^cite_note-437] entitling him to use the [honorific prefix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_honorifics) "[The Right Honourable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Right_Honourable)".[^cite_note-438]
